<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt">12. <U>Profit Sharing and 401(k) Plan</U>
</DIV>



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">We have a profit sharing plan that includes a salary reduction feature established under the
Internal Revenue Code Section 401(k) covering substantially all employees. Company
contributions to the profit sharing plan are made in cash and determined at the discretion of
the Board of Directors. As discussed in Note 3, an additional
contribution for 2005 will be made to the
401(k) plan in conjunction with a modification to the Company&#146;s stock option program. For the
years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, 2004 and 2003, amounts charged to expense for this plan totaled
$10.9&nbsp;million, $5.5&nbsp;million, and $3.1&nbsp;million, respectively.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt">13. <U>Leasing Joint Venture</U>
</DIV>



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">CDW-L was a joint venture that was 50&nbsp;percent owned by each of CDWCC, a wholly-owned subsidiary
of the Company, and First Portland Corporation (&#147;FIRSTCORP&#148;), an unrelated third party leasing
company. In
a transaction that was effective August&nbsp;1, 2004, CDWCC sold its 50&nbsp;percent interest in CDW-L to
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">50
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">FIRSTCORP for $2.7&nbsp;million. The sale of $2.4&nbsp;million of net assets, including $5.0&nbsp;million in
cash, resulted in a gain of $0.3&nbsp;million which is included in income from operations.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">In accordance with FASB Interpretation No.&nbsp;46 (revised December&nbsp;2003), &#147;Consolidation of
Variable Interest Entities, an interpretation of ARB 51,&#148; we consolidated CDW-L on December&nbsp;31,
2003. CDW-L&#146;s results of operations subsequent to December&nbsp;31, 2003 and through the date of
sale are included in our statement of income with a minority interest for FIRSTCORP&#146;s 50
percent interest in this joint venture reflected in other expense, net. CDW-L had a $40
million financing commitment from a financial institution, of which $1.5&nbsp;million was
outstanding at December&nbsp;31, 2003. During the first quarter of 2004, the balance of $1.5
million was repaid and the financing commitment was terminated.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt">14. <U>Contingencies</U>
</DIV>



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">On September&nbsp;9, 2003, CDW completed the purchase of certain assets of Bridgeport Holdings,
Inc., Micro Warehouse, Inc., Micro Warehouse, Inc. of Ohio, and Micro Warehouse Gov/Ed, Inc.
(collectively, &#147;Micro Warehouse&#148;). On September&nbsp;10, 2003, Micro Warehouse filed voluntary
petitions for relief under chapter 11 of title 11 of the United States Code (the &#147;Bankruptcy
Code&#148;) in the United States Bankruptcy Court for the District of Delaware (Case No.&nbsp;03-12825).
On January&nbsp;20, 2004, the Official Committee of Unsecured Creditors (the &#147;Committee&#148;) appointed
in the Micro Warehouse bankruptcy proceedings filed a motion with the court seeking the
production of certain documents for review and certain representatives of CDW for depositions.
On February&nbsp;12, 2004, the Bankruptcy Court entered an order approving a stipulation between the
Committee and CDW whereby CDW consented to the Committee&#146;s production requests. Pursuant to
the stipulation, CDW produced the requested documents and certain CDW representatives were
deposed. In a subsequent filing with the Bankruptcy Court, the Committee stated its belief
that the Micro Warehouse estate has a claim against CDW for a transfer of assets for less than
reasonably equivalent value arising from the sale of such assets to CDW. The Bankruptcy Court
confirmed a plan of distribution with respect to Micro Warehouse which became effective on
October&nbsp;14, 2004. In connection therewith, any such claim that the estate had against CDW was
transferred to the Bridgeport Holdings, Inc. Liquidating Trust (the &#147;Liquidating Trust&#148;). On
March&nbsp;3, 2005, the Liquidating Trust filed a civil claim against CDW in the United States
Bankruptcy Court for the District of Delaware. The Liquidating Trust alleges that CDW did not
pay reasonably equivalent value for the assets it acquired from Micro Warehouse and seeks to
have CDW&#146;s &#147;purchase of Micro Warehouse&#148; set aside and an amount of damages, to be determined
at trial, paid to it. CDW believes that it paid reasonably equivalent value for the assets it
acquired from Micro Warehouse and believes that the outcome of this claim will not have a
material adverse effect on CDW&#146;s financial condition. It is not possible for CDW to estimate a
range of any possible loss that could result from this litigation.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">From time to time, customers of CDW file voluntary petitions for reorganization under the
United States bankruptcy laws. In such cases, certain pre-petition payments received by CDW
could be considered preference items and subject to return to the bankruptcy administrator. CDW
believes that the final resolution of these preference items will not have a material adverse
effect on its financial condition.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">In addition, CDW is party to legal proceedings that arise from time to time, both with respect
to specific transactions, such as the purchase of certain assets from Micro Warehouse described
above, and in the ordinary course of our business. We do not believe that any currently
pending or threatened litigation will have a material adverse effect on our financial
condition. Litigation, however, involves uncertainties and it is possible that the eventual
outcome of litigation could adversely affect our results of operations for a particular period.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt">15. <U>Segment Information</U>
</DIV>



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">We have two operating segments: corporate sector, which is primarily comprised of business
customers, but also includes consumers, and public sector, which is comprised of federal, state
and local government entities, educational institutions and healthcare customers. In
accordance with Statement of Financial
Accounting Standards No.&nbsp;131, &#147;Disclosure about Segments of an Enterprise and Related
Information,&#148; the
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">51
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">internal organization that is used by management for making operating
decisions and assessing performance is the source of our reportable segments.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">In the first quarter of 2005, we revised the operating segments which reflect the basis for
making operating decisions and assessing performance. Under the revised structure, centralized
logistics and headquarters functions that were formerly provided by the corporate sector
segment to the public sector segment were separated from the corporate sector segment. The
logistics functions include purchasing, distribution, and fulfillment services to support both
the corporate and public sector segments, and costs and intercompany charges associated with
the logistics function are fully allocated to both of the operating segments based on a percent
of sales. The centralized headquarters functions provide services in areas such as accounting,
information technology, marketing, legal, and coworker services. Certain of the headquarters
function costs that are not allocated to the operating segments are included under the heading
of &#147;Headquarters/Other&#148; in the tables below.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">In July&nbsp;2005, the Company announced the creation of a dedicated healthcare sales team. In
creating this team, we consolidated healthcare accounts from across our entire sales
organization and transferred these accounts to the new team in the public sector group. This
new team focuses on IT solutions addressing the unique needs of a range of customers within the
healthcare field. For financial reporting purposes, results of operations and assets related
to healthcare customers are reported as part of the public sector segment.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">The accounting policies of the segments are the same as those described in Note 2, &#147;Summary of
Significant Accounting Policies.&#148; We allocate resources to and evaluate performance of our
segments based on both sales and operating income.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">We have restated the prior year segment information to conform to our revised segment reporting
structure. The following tables present information about our reportable segments (in
thousands):
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 2%">Our assets are primarily managed by our headquarters functions, including all cash, cash
equivalents, and marketable securities, inventory, and the majority of all property and
equipment. As a result, capital expenditures and related depreciation are immaterial for the
two operating segments. The operating segments&#146; assets consist principally of accounts
receivable.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 2%">No single customer accounted for more than 1% of net sales in fiscal years 2005, 2004 or 2003.
During 2005, approximately 2% of our sales were to customers outside of the continental United
States, primarily in Canada.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt">16. <U>Share Repurchase Programs</U>
</DIV>



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">Since 1998, we have repurchased a total of 13,483,400 shares of our common stock at a total
cost of $649.2&nbsp;million under various share repurchase programs authorized by our Board of
Directors. Our current program authorizing the repurchase of 4,529,600 shares was approved by
our Board of Directors in April&nbsp;2005. Share repurchases may be made from time to time in both
open market and private transactions, as conditions warrant. The current repurchase program is
expected to remain in effect through April&nbsp;2007, unless earlier terminated by the Board or
completed. The following table presents share repurchases for the years ended December&nbsp;31,
2005, 2004 and 2003 (dollars in thousands):

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt">17. <U>Public Offering of Common Shares</U>
</DIV>



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">In May&nbsp;2003, Gregory C. Zeman, former director and vice chairman of the Company, and Daniel B.
Kass, former director and executive vice president of the Company, sold a total of 1,108,864
shares of common stock. We did not receive any proceeds from the sale of shares and the number
of outstanding common shares was not impacted. The shares sold by Mr.&nbsp;Zeman and Mr.&nbsp;Kass were
acquired from Michael P. Krasny, the chairman emeritus, principal shareholder and a director of
the Company through the exercise of options previously granted to them pursuant to the MPK
Stock Option Plan. The exercise of options by Mr.&nbsp;Zeman and Mr.&nbsp;Kass resulted in the
realization by the Company of an income tax benefit of approximately $17.7&nbsp;million in 2003, of
which approximately $0.3&nbsp;million had been previously recorded to deferred taxes. We recorded
the incremental tax benefit of $17.4&nbsp;million as an increase to paid-in capital. In addition,
we recorded incremental payroll tax expense related to the option exercise of approximately
$0.7&nbsp;million, which reduced diluted earnings per share in 2003 by less than $0.01 per share.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt">18. <U>Micro Warehouse Transactions</U>
</DIV>



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">During September&nbsp;2003, we purchased selected U.S. assets and the Canadian operations of Micro
Warehouse, a reseller of computers, software and peripheral products. The U.S. transaction,
completed on September&nbsp;9, 2003, was accounted for as a purchase of assets, with the $20.0
million purchase price allocated to the assets purchased, including inventory, fixed assets and
customer lists, based upon their fair values at the date of purchase. Subsequent to the
completion of the U.S. transaction, sales made by former members of the Micro Warehouse U.S.
sales force who joined CDW in conjunction with this transaction, along with the associated
costs, are included in the accompanying consolidated financial statements. The Canadian
transaction, completed on September&nbsp;23, 2003, was accounted for as the purchase of a business
and, accordingly, the results of operations of the acquired business subsequent to the date of
purchase are included in the accompanying consolidated financial statements, and the assumed
assets and liabilities were recorded based upon their fair values at the date of purchase. The
Canadian operations were purchased for $2.7&nbsp;million.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">During the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2003, we recorded $22.3&nbsp;million (pre-tax) of transaction and
integration expenses associated with these transactions. These expenses were primarily
comprised of severance and outplacement costs, payroll expenses for former Micro Warehouse
employees performing transition services, customer satisfaction expenses, customer
communications and advertising expenses, legal and accounting advisory fees and a reserve
established for the equipment in a Wilmington, Ohio distribution center leased by Micro
Warehouse as discussed below. These expenses are included in cost of sales ($0.3&nbsp;million),
selling and administrative expenses ($20.2&nbsp;million), net advertising expenses ($1.5&nbsp;million)
and other expense ($0.3&nbsp;million) in the Consolidated Statements of Income in 2003.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">During the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2004, we recorded $3.9&nbsp;million (pre-tax) of transaction and
integration expenses associated with these transactions. These expenses were primarily
comprised of payroll expenses for former Micro Warehouse employees performing transition
services, legal fees and an adjustment to the reserve established for the equipment in the
Wilmington, Ohio distribution center leased by Micro
Warehouse as discussed below. These expenses are included in selling and administrative
expenses in the Consolidated Statements of Income in 2004.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">54
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt; margin-left: 3%">In February&nbsp;2004, we purchased the equipment in the Wilmington, Ohio distribution center leased
by Micro Warehouse and forfeited leasing the facility in exchange for $8.25&nbsp;million. During
2003, we recorded a $5.0&nbsp;million reserve related to the purchased equipment in accordance with
FASB Interpretation No.&nbsp;45, &#147;Guarantor&#146;s Accounting and Disclosure Requirements for Guarantees,
Including Indirect Guarantees of Indebtedness of Others.&#148; At March&nbsp;31, 2004, we increased this
reserve by $2.0&nbsp;million due to a change in the scenarios used in estimating the Company&#146;s
exposure. During the third quarter of 2004, substantially all of this equipment was liquidated
at values in line with our expectations.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Item&nbsp;9.</B>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<u><B>Changes in and Disagreements with Accountants on Accounting
and Financial Disclosure.</B></u>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;None.
</DIV>
<DIV align="left">
<A name="115"></A>
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Item&nbsp;9A.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</B><u><B>Controls and Procedures.</B></u>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B>Evaluation of Disclosure Controls and Procedures</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;
As required by Rule 13a-15(b) under the Securities Exchange Act of
1934, as amended (the &#147;Exchange Act&#148;), the Company&#146;s
management carried out an evaluation, with the participation of the
Company&#146;s Chief Executive Officer and Chief Financial Officer,
of the effectiveness of the Company&#146;s disclosure controls and
procedures (as defined in Rules 13a-15(e) and 15d-15(e) under the Exchange Act) as
of December 31, 2005. Based upon that evaluation, the Chief
Executive Officer and Chief Financial Officer concluded that, as of
December 31, 2005, the Company&#146;s disclosure controls and procedures
were effective to ensure that the information required to be disclosed
by the Company (including its consolidated subsidiaries) in the
reports the Company files or submits under the Exchange Act is
recorded, processed, summarized and reported within the time
periods specified in the Securities and Exchange Commission&#146;s rules and forms.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Design and Evaluation of Internal Control Over Financial Reporting</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We have included Management&#146;s Report on Internal Control Over Financial Reporting as part of
this Annual Report on Form 10-K. This report, which is found on page 28 herein, contains
management&#146;s assessment of the effectiveness of the Company&#146;s internal control over financial
reporting as of December&nbsp;31, 2005. The Company&#146;s independent registered public accounting firm has
audited management&#146;s assessment of the effectiveness of the Company&#146;s internal control over
financial reporting as of December&nbsp;31, 2005 and the effectiveness of our internal control over
financial reporting as of December&nbsp;31, 2005 as stated in its report which is included on page 29
herein.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Changes in Internal Control Over Financial Reporting</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There was no change in the Company&#146;s internal control over financial reporting that
occurred during the Company&#146;s fiscal quarter ended December&nbsp;31, 2005 that materially affected,
or is reasonably likely to materially affect, the Company&#146;s internal control over financial
reporting.
</DIV>
<DIV align="left">
<A name="116"></A>
